As before, we aim to extend our parameterization of such measures by representing them as pushforwards through suitable transport maps. Fixing
\rho=\mathcal{N}(0,I)
(or more generally, any product measure with a density), we can associate a Knothe–Rosenblatt (KR) map to any distribution in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
via the following recursive representation for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
:

z_{i}=T_{i}(x_{i}\mid z_{\pa(i)})\,,

where
\pa(i)
denotes the parent node(s) of
i
. In analogy with
Section

3.1
, we denote by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
the set of KR maps induced by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

However, for a general graph
\mathcal{G}
, the set
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
may not be convex. Indeed, the argument in
Proposition

3.1
relies on the injectivity of the map
\lambda T_{1}^{1}+(1-\lambda)T_{1}^{2}
for any two
T^{1},T^{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
, which cannot be easily verified for general
\mathcal{G}
. For example, let
\mathcal{G}
be a three‐node Markov chain
z_{1}\to z_{2}\to z_{3}
, and let
\rho=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{3})
. Define
T^{1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\coloneqq\bigl(x_{1},\;x_{2},\;x_{3}\bigr)
and
T^{2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\coloneqq\bigl(x_{1},\;x_{1},\;x_{2}\bigr)
, then
T^{1},T^{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
yet
\frac{1}{2}T^{1}+\frac{1}{2}T^{2}\not\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Moreover, obtaining regularity results for the minimizers is also challenging due to the complex structure of the problem. As a result, a principled approach to computation based on the polyhedral approximation is more costly, if not intractable, since the KR map
T_{i}
(as a function of
x
) depends on all nodes on the path to the tree root prior to node
i
.

4.2
A suitably nice subset of transport maps

A possible remedy is to restrict the minimization to a “nice” subset of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
, making the problem more tractable. Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}
as the set of transport maps with the following structure:

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\bigl\{T:x\mapsto(T_{i}(x_{i};x_{\pa(i)}))_{i\in[d]}\bigm|T_{i}(\cdot\,;x_{\pa(i)})\text{ is strictly increasing},\,\forall i\in[d]\bigr\}\,.

Recall that for any
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
, the component maps
T_{i}
take the form
T_{i}(\cdot\mid z_{\pa(i)})
, where
z_{\pa(i)}
depends on all the nodes lying on the path from node
i
to the root. Thus, we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{G}}
, with equality achieved if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is a star graph. We introduce the surrogate structured variational inference (Surro-SSVI) problem as follows:

T^{\star}\coloneqq\argmin_{T\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}}\mathsf{KL}\left(T_{\#}\rho\parallel\pi\right).

(
\mathsf{Surro}
-
\mathsf{SSVI}
)

Observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}
is convex. Thus, by the same argument as in
Theorem

3.2
, we can show that (

Surro

-

SSVI

) admits a unique solution under (
SLC
).

However, the minimizing set is less expressive than that of the full SSVI problem (

T

-

SSVI

), as it imposes additional graphical constraints on the distributions. For example, consider the case where the graph is a Markov chain given by
z_{1}\to z_{2}\to\dots\to z_{d}
, and let
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})\sim T_{\#}\rho
for some
T\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}
. Then,
Z_{1}
is independent of
Z_{i}
for any
i\geq 3
. In particular, if we restrict
T\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}
to be a linear map, then
T_{\#}\rho
is again a Gaussian distribution. Let
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denote its covariance matrix, then
\Sigma_{ij}=0
whenever
|j-i|\geq 2
, implying that
\Sigma
is necessarily sparse. In contrast, the general structured variational inference framework typically assumes that the
precision
matrix
\Sigma^{-1}
is sparse, rather than
\Sigma
itself.

The extension of our results under (

Surro

-

SSVI

), and more generally to arbitrary tree graphs, is beyond the scope of this paper.

Appendix A
Proofs from Section
2

We recall that a function
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty]
is called
lower semianalytic
if the sets
\{f<c\}
are analytic for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
, where a subset of
\mathbb{R}
is called analytic if it is the image of a Borel subset of a Polish space under a Borel mapping. Any Borel function is lower semianalytic and a lower semianalytic function is universally measurable.
We refer readers to
14
for the terminology used in the following proof, including analytic sets, lower semianalytic functions, and universally measurable functions.

Proof of
Proposition

2.1
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
,
\mu(\mathrm{d}z)=\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\,\mu_{-1}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})
with
\mu_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}
.
The lower bound

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\inf_{\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})}\Bigl\{\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{1}\parallel\pi_{1}\right)+\int\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\nu\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\Bigr\}\eqcolon V_{\rm iter}

follows trivially from the KL chain rule.

To see the converse inequality, for every
\epsilon>0
, we want to show that

\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)\leq V_{\rm iter}+\epsilon\,.

Consider

h_{\rm val}(z_{1})\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\nu\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)\,.

(32)

Then the minimizer to the outer problem is given by

\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\propto e^{-h_{\rm val}(z_{1})}\,\pi_{1}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\,.

Since
\nu
varies over a Borel subset of a Polish space,
z_{1}\mapsto\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})
is Borel with values in a Polish space, and
\mathsf{KL}\left(\cdot\parallel\cdot\right)
is jointly Borel and non-negative. This implies that
h_{\rm val}
is lower semianalytic
(
14
, Proposition 7.47)
. Then, the selection theorem for lower semianalytic functions
(see, e.g.,
14
, Proposition 7.50(a))
yields a universally measurable function
z_{1}\mapsto\nu^{\epsilon}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}
such that

\mathsf{KL}\left(\nu^{\epsilon}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)\leq\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\nu\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)+\epsilon=h_{\rm val}(z_{1})+\epsilon\,.

Let
\pi^{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}z)\coloneqq\rmp^{\star}_{1}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\,\nu^{\epsilon}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})
. Then,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)\leq\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\epsilon}\parallel\pi\right)\leq V_{\rm iter}+\epsilon\,.

As
\epsilon
is chosen arbitrarily, we conclude the statement as desired.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.2
.

Under (
SLC
),
h_{\rm val}(z_{1})
attains a unique solution, denoted by
\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})
; and
z_{1}\mapsto\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})
is universally measurable
(see, e.g.,
14
, Proposition 7.50(b))
. Therefore, after modifying on a null set with respect to
\pi_{1}
,
z_{1}\mapsto\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})
is a Borel-measurable stochastic kernel
(e.g.,
14
, Lemma 7.28)
so that

\pi^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z)\propto e^{-h_{\rm val}(z_{1})}\,\pi_{1}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\,\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}\,.

This concludes the existence and uniqueness of the solution to (

SSVI

).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.4
.

The SSVI problem is equivalent to finding the optimal star-separable map
T^{\star}
in (

T

-

SSVI

). Recall that the Wasserstein gradient of
\mu\mapsto\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)
at
\mu
is
\nabla\log\frac{\mu}{\pi}

(
3
;
29
, see e.g.,)
. This implies the first-order optimality condition

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl\langle\nabla\log\frac{\pi^{\star}(Z)}{\pi(Z)},\,T(Z)-Z\Bigr\rangle=0\,,

(33)

for any map
T
such that
T_{\#}\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
, i.e., for
T\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
.

In particular, (
33
) holds for star-separable maps of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{i}(z)

\displaystyle=z+\left[0,\dotsc,0,T_{i}(z_{i};z_{1})-z_{i},0,\dotsc,0\right]

with the convention that
\mathbb{T}^{1}(z)=\left[T_{1}(z_{1}),0,\dotsc,0\right]
. Applying (
33
) to each
\mathbb{T}^{i}
,

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\partial_{1}\log\frac{\pi^{\star}(Z)}{\pi(Z)}\left(T_{1}(Z_{1})-Z_{1}\right)\Bigr]=0\,,

(34)

and for
i=2,\ldots,d

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\partial_{i}\log\frac{\pi^{\star}(Z)}{\pi(Z)}\left(T_{i}\left(Z_{i};Z_{1}\right)-Z_{i}\right)\Bigr]=0\,.

After decomposing
\pi^{\star}
, we have that for
i\neq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\left(\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(Z_{i}\mid Z_{1})+\partial_{i}V(Z)\right)\left(T_{i}(Z_{i};Z_{1})-Z_{i}\right)\right]=0\,.

Since the function
T_{i}
is an arbitrary monotone function in the first argument and an arbitrary function in the second argument, the closed linear span of the family of functions
(x,y)\mapsto T_{i}(x;y)-x
contains
C_{\rm b}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the set of all bounded continuous functions.
Therefore, we obtain a simple identity

\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]=0\,,

for
\pi^{\star}
-a.e.
(z_{1},z_{i})
. Integrating the above display on both sides with respect to
z_{i}
and noting that the order of differentiation and conditional expectation can be exchanged as
V
is convex, we have

\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})\propto\exp\left(-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]\right).

By the same argument as above, (
34
) can be written as

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(Z_{1})+\sum_{j\geq 2}\partial_{1}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(Z_{j}\mid Z_{1})+\partial_{1}V(Z)\Bigr)\,(T_{1}(Z_{1})-Z_{1})\Bigr]=0\,.

Using the tower property, the above display becomes

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\Bigl(\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(Z_{1})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\sum_{j\geq 2}\partial_{1}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(Z_{j}\mid Z_{1})+\partial_{1}V(Z)\Bigm|Z_{1}\Bigr]\Bigr)\,(T_{1}(Z_{1})-Z_{1})\Bigr]=0\,.

Hence,

\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\sum_{j\geq 2}\partial_{1}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(Z_{j}\mid Z_{1})+\partial_{1}V(Z)\Bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1}\Bigr]=0\,,

(35)

for
\rmp^{\star}
-a.e.
z_{1}
. To further simplify, we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\partial_{1}\sum_{j\geq 2}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(Z_{j}\mid Z_{1})\Bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(Z_{j}\mid Z_{1})\Bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1}\right]

(36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 2}\int\frac{\partial_{1}\rmq_{j}^{\star}(z_{j}\mid z_{1})}{\rmq_{j}^{\star}(z_{j}\mid z_{1})}\,\rmq_{j}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mid z_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 2}\partial_{1}\int\rmq_{j}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mid z_{1})=0\,.

Therefore,

\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}\right]=0\,,

for
\rmp^{\star}
-a.e.
z_{1}
. Integrating both sides with respect to
z_{1}
yields

\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})\propto\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{z_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=s\right]\mathrm{d}s\right),

(37)

as desired.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.6
.

Since
L_{V}\vee(L_{V}^{\prime}/2)I_{d-1}\succeq(\nabla^{2}V)_{-1}\succeq\ell_{V}^{\prime}\land\ell_{V}I_{d-1}
and its Schur complement

\displaystyle L_{V}\vee(L_{V}^{\prime}/2)

\displaystyle\geq\partial_{11}V-(\nabla_{-1,1}^{2}V)^{\top}\,((\nabla^{2}V)_{-1})^{-1}\,(\nabla_{-1,1}^{2}V)

\displaystyle\geq\partial_{11}V-\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\left(\partial_{1j}V\right)^{2}\geq\ell_{V}^{\prime}\land\ell_{V}\,,

where the last inequality follows from (
RD
).
Applying the Schur’s complement theorem
(
119
, Theorem 1.6)
yields the desired result.
∎

We recall the following result by
93
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD
) hold. For any
z_{1},z_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,

W_{2}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime})\right)\leq\frac{\left\|\sqrt{\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\,|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|\,.

Proof.

The assumptions in
93
are satisfied by
Lemma

2.6
, and therefore

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\,\|\nabla_{-1}V(z_{1},\cdot)-\nabla_{-1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|\sqrt{\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\,|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|\,.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.8
.

Differentiating the self-consistency equation in
Theorem

2.4
for
\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{i}^{2}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}\left(z_{i}\mid z_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\int\partial_{i}V(z_{1},z_{i},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{ii}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},\,Z_{i}=z_{i}\right].

In the last line, we can interchange differentiation and integration because the map
z_{i}\mapsto\partial_{i}V(z_{1},z_{i},z_{-\{1,i\}})
is Lipschitz, as
\ell_{V}\leq\partial_{ii}V\leq L_{V}
. Thus,
\ell_{V}\leq-\partial_{i}^{2}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})\leq L_{V}
.

On the other hand, by the self-consistency equation for
\rmp^{\star}
in
Theorem

2.4
, we obtain

-\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}\right],

and thus,

\displaystyle-\partial_{1}^{2}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\int\partial_{1}V(z_{1},z_{-1})\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{z_{1}^{\prime}\to z_{1}}\frac{\int\partial_{1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{-1})\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1}^{\prime})-\int\partial_{1}V(z_{1},z_{-1})\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})}{|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{z_{1}^{\prime}\to z_{1}}\frac{\int\left(\partial_{1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{-1})-\partial_{1}V(z_{1},z_{-1})\right)\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})}{|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\lim_{z_{1}^{\prime}\to z_{1}}\frac{\int\partial_{1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{-1})\left(\rmq^{\star}(z_{-1}\mid z_{1}^{\prime})-\rmq^{\star}(z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\right)\mathrm{d}z_{-1}}{|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|}}_{\eqqcolon A}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{11}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}\right]+A\,.

As
z_{-1}\mapsto\partial_{1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{-1})
is
\|\sqrt{\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}
-Lipschitz, the dual formulation for the
1
-Wasserstein distance
(
100
, see e.g.,)
yields that for each
z_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\int\partial_{1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{-1})\left(\rmq^{\star}\left(z_{-1}\mid z_{1}^{\prime}\right)-\rmq^{\star}\left(z_{-1}\mid z_{1}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\Bigr\rvert

(38)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\|\bigl({\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}}\bigr)^{1/2}\Bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}\,W_{1}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime})\right).

As
W_{1}\leq W_{2}
, we can apply
Lemma

A.1
and obtain

\displaystyle W_{1}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq W_{2}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\,\|\nabla_{-1}V(z_{1},\cdot)-\nabla_{-1}V(z_{1}^{\prime},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}^{\prime}))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\|\sqrt{\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\,|z_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime}|\,.

Combining with (
38
) gives that

|A|\leq\frac{\left\|\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\,.

Therefore,

-\partial_{1}^{2}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{11}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}\right]+A\geq\ell_{V}^{\prime}

as we assumed that

\partial_{11}V-\frac{\left\|\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\geq\ell_{V}^{\prime}\,.

Similarly,

-\partial_{1}^{2}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{11}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}\right]+\frac{\left\|\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}}\leq L_{V}^{\prime}\,.

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.9
.

The proof builds on a collection of functional inequalities, such as the log-Sobolev inequality
(
100
, Theorem 9.9)
, the Talagrand inequality
(
77
, Theorem 1)
, and the Poincaré inequality
(
8
, Corollary 4.8.2)
.

First, by the optimal representation of
\pi^{\star}
and (two applications of) the log-Sobolev inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{KL}\left(\rmp^{\star}\parallel\pi_{1}\right)+\int\mathsf{KL}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})

(39)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\ell_{V}^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})-\partial_{1}\log\pi_{1}(z_{1})\right|^{2}\,\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\ell_{V}}\sum_{i\geq 2}\int\left|-\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})-\partial_{i}V(z)\right|^{2}\,\pi^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z)\,.

For each
i\geq 2
, the self-consistency equation (
14
) implies that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1},Z_{i}]=-\partial_{i}\log q_{i}^{\star}(Z_{i}\mid Z_{1})\,.

Then as
\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}\mid z_{1})
is
\ell_{V}
-log-concave by
Theorem

2.8
and
\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}
, the Poincaré inequality yields that

\displaystyle\int\left|-\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})-\partial_{i}V(z)\right|^{2}\,\pi^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z)

(40)

\displaystyle=\int{\rm Var}_{\pi^{\star}}(\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i})\,\pi_{1i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1},\mathrm{d}z_{i})\leq\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}{\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[|\partial_{ij}V(Z)|^{2}]}\,.

On the other hand, we note that

\partial_{1}\log\pi_{1}(z_{1})=\partial_{1}\log\int\exp\left(-V(z)\right)\mathrm{d}z_{-1}=-\int\partial_{1}V(z)\,\pi_{-1}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\,.

Then, combining with the self-consistency equation (
14
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int\left|\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})-\partial_{1}\log\pi_{1}(z_{1})\right|^{2}\,\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad=\int\Bigl\lvert\int\partial_{1}V(z)\,(\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})-\pi_{-1}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1}))\Bigr\rvert^{2}\,\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\bigl\lVert\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\bigr\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\int W_{1}^{2}\left(\rmq^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1}),\,\pi_{-1}(\mathrm{d}z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\right)\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})\,.

The last line uses the fact that
\partial_{1}V(z_{1},\cdot)
is
\|\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{1/2}
–Lipschitz.

By (
R
) and (
RD
), we have

L_{V}^{\prime}/2-\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\left(\partial_{1j}V\right)^{2}\geq\partial_{11}V-\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\left(\partial_{1j}V\right)^{2}\geq\ell_{V}^{\prime}\,.

Hence,
\sum_{i\geq 2}(\partial_{1i}V)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\ell_{V}L_{V}^{\prime}-\ell_{V}\ell_{V}^{\prime}<\infty
.

Thus, by the
T_{1}
-transport inequality and then the log-Sobolev inequality,

\displaystyle\int\left|\partial_{1}\log\rmp^{\star}(z_{1})-\partial_{1}\log\pi_{1}(z_{1})\right|^{2}\,\rmp^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\frac{1}{2}\ell_{V}L_{V}^{\prime}-\ell_{V}\ell_{V}^{\prime}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\sum_{i\geq 2}\int\left|-\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})-\partial_{i}V(z)\right|^{2}\,\pi^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\frac{1}{2}L_{V}^{\prime}-\ell_{V}^{\prime}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\sum_{i\geq 2}\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[|\partial_{ij}V(Z)|^{2}]\,,

where the last line follows from (
40
).

Combining all the above bounds, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\frac{1}{2\ell_{V}^{2}}+\frac{L_{V}^{\prime}}{4\ell_{V}^{\prime}\ell_{V}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\ell_{V}^{2}}\bigr)\sum_{i\geq 2}\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\bigl[(\partial_{ij}V(Z))^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{L_{V}^{\prime}}{2\ell_{V}^{\prime}\ell_{V}^{2}}\sum_{i\geq 2}\sum_{j>i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\bigl[(\partial_{ij}V(Z))^{2}\bigr]\,.

∎

A.1
Proofs for Examples

We first recall the following lemma, which provides the explicit expression for the KL divergence between two Gaussian distributions.

Lemma A.2

(KL divergence between Gaussians)
.

Consider
p_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{0},\Sigma_{0})
,
p_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})
with
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(p_{0}\parallel p_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\log\dfrac{\det\Sigma_{1}}{\det\Sigma_{0}}+\dfrac{1}{2}({\rm tr}(\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\Sigma_{0})-d)+\dfrac{1}{2}(\mu_{0}-\mu_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{0}-\mu_{1})\,.

Proof of
Theorem

2.10
.

By
Proposition

2.1
and by
Theorem

3.2
, we know that (
3
) admits a unique optimizer given by

\pi^{\star}(z)\propto e^{-h(z_{1})}\,\pi_{1}(z_{1})\,\rmq^{\star}(z_{-1}\mid z_{1})\,,

where
\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})
is the minimizer for

h_{\rm val}(z_{1})=\inf_{\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{ac}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\nu\parallel\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right)\,.

Since
\pi=\mathcal{N}(m,\Sigma)
, denote by
m_{-1}=(m_{2},\ldots,m_{d})^{\top}
,
\sigma_{ij}=\Sigma_{ij}
,
\Sigma_{-1,-1}=(\sigma_{ij})_{2\leq i,j\leq d}
, and
\Sigma_{-1,1}=(\sigma_{12},\ldots,\sigma_{1d})^{\top}
, we know that
\pi_{-1}(\cdot\mid z_{1})=\mathcal{N}(m_{z_{1}},\Sigma_{|1})
where

\displaystyle m_{z_{1}}

\displaystyle=m_{-1}+(\sigma_{11})^{-1}\Sigma_{-1,1}(z_{1}-m_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{|1}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{-1,-1}-(\sigma_{11})^{-1}\Sigma_{-1,1}\Sigma_{-1,1}^{\top}=((\Sigma^{-1})_{-1,-1})^{-1}\,.

Therefore,
64
yields that
\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})=\mathcal{N}(m_{z_{1}},\Sigma_{|1}^{\star})
where
\Sigma_{|1}^{*}={{\rm diag}(\Sigma_{|1}^{-1})}^{-1}
;
and by
Lemma

A.2
, we derive that
h_{\rm val}(z_{1})\equiv C
for some
C>0
. Thus, we may conclude that
\pi^{\star}
is also Gaussian. Finally, (
16
) can be derived using the tower property of expectation.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

2.11
.

By
Theorem

2.10
and the results by
64
, we know that both
\pi^{\star}
and
\bar{\pi}
are normal distributions with mean zero and covariance matrices
\Sigma^{\star},\bar{\Sigma}
, respectively. Then
Lemma

A.2
implies that

\mathsf{KL}\left(\bar{\pi}\parallel\pi\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\bar{\Sigma}}{\det\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\,\bigl({\rm tr}(\bar{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1})-d\bigr)\,,

and

\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\Sigma^{\star}}{\det\Sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\,\bigl({\rm tr}(\Sigma^{\star}\Sigma^{-1})-d\bigr)\,.

Thus,

\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)-\mathsf{KL}\left(\bar{\pi}\parallel\pi\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\det\Sigma^{\star}}{\det\bar{\Sigma}}+\frac{1}{2}\tr((\Sigma^{\star}-\bar{\Sigma})\Sigma^{-1})\,.

(41)

By (
16
), we can express the difference between
\Sigma^{\star}
and
\bar{\Sigma}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\star}-\bar{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{11}-(\sigma^{11})^{-1}&\sigma_{12}&\cdots&\sigma_{1i}&\cdots&\sigma_{1d}\\[2.5pt]
\sigma_{12}&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}\sigma_{1d}}{\sigma_{11}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{1i}&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}\sigma_{12}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}\sigma_{1d}}{\sigma_{11}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\sigma_{1d}&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}\sigma_{12}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon\Sigma+\tilde{\Sigma}\,,

where

\tilde{\Sigma}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}-(\sigma^{11})^{-1}&0&\cdots&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{22}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{2i}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{12}\sigma_{1d}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{2d}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}\sigma_{12}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{i2}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{ii}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}\sigma_{1d}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{id}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}\sigma_{12}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{d2}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{di}&\cdots&\dfrac{\sigma_{1d}^{2}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{dd}\end{pmatrix}.

Then

\displaystyle\tr\bigl((\Sigma^{\star}-\bar{\Sigma})\Sigma^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tr(I+\tilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1})=d+\tr(\tilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{-1})

\displaystyle=d-(\sigma^{11})^{-1}\sigma^{11}+\sum_{i\geq 2}\sum_{j\geq 2}\bigl(\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}\sigma_{1j}}{\sigma_{11}}-\sigma_{ij}\bigr)\,\sigma^{ji}

\displaystyle=d-1+\sum_{i\geq 2}\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}\sum_{j\geq 2}\sigma_{1j}\sigma^{ji}-\sum_{i\geq 2}\sum_{j\geq 2}\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ji}\,.

By the fact that
\Sigma\Sigma^{-1}=I
, we have

\displaystyle\tr\bigl((\Sigma^{\star}-\bar{\Sigma})\Sigma^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d-1+\sum_{i\geq 2}\dfrac{\sigma_{1i}}{\sigma_{11}}\,(-\sigma_{11}\sigma^{1i})-\sum_{i\geq 2}(1-\sigma_{i1}\sigma^{1i})=0\,.

To compute the determinant of
\Sigma^{\star}
, we observe that: letting
U_{i}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&v_{i}^{\top}\\
0&I_{d-1}\end{pmatrix}
, where
v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
with its
(i-1)
-th entry being
-\sigma_{11}^{-1}\sigma_{1i}
and all the others being zero,

\bigl(\prod_{i=2}^{d}U_{i}\bigr)^{\top}\Sigma^{\star}\bigl(\prod_{i=2}^{d}U_{i}\bigr)=\diag(\sigma_{11},(\sigma^{22})^{-1},\dotsc,(\sigma^{dd})^{-1})\,.

Since
\det U_{i}=1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\det\Sigma^{\star}=\sigma_{11}\prod_{i=2}^{d}(\sigma^{ii})^{-1}\,.

On the other hand, we know that
\det\bar{\Sigma}=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\sigma^{ii})^{-1}
, thus

\dfrac{\det\Sigma^{\star}}{\det\bar{\Sigma}}=\dfrac{\sigma_{11}}{(\sigma^{11})^{-1}}\,.

(42)

Substitute (
42
) into (
41
), and by the fact that
\sigma_{11}\geq(\sigma^{11})^{-1}
, we obtain

\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)-\mathsf{KL}\left(\bar{\pi}\parallel\pi\right)=-\dfrac{1}{2}\log\Bigl(\dfrac{\sigma_{11}}{(\sigma^{11})^{-1}}\Bigr)\leq 0\,.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.12
.

Recall that the target measure has the form

V_{\rm loc}(\vartheta,\beta)=\rmg(\vartheta)-w^{\top}\beta+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\psi(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)}{c(\sigma)}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)\,.

To verify the conditions in
Theorem

2.9
, we compute
\partial^{2}_{\vartheta}V_{\rm loc}
, the principal minor
\nabla^{2}_{\beta}V_{\rm loc}
, and the cross terms. By the assumptions on
\rmg
and
\varrho
, we have

\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}V_{\rm loc}(\vartheta,\beta)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)+\rmg^{\prime\prime}(\vartheta)\leq dR+L\,.

Letting
D
be a
d\times d
diagonal matrix with
D_{jj}=\varrho^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)\geq 0
, the principal minor of
\nabla^{2}V_{\rm loc}
satisfies

\nabla_{\beta}^{2}V_{\rm loc}(\vartheta,\beta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)+D\succeq bA\succeq baI\,,

as
A=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\succeq aI
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}\geq b
. Finally, the cross-term is
\partial_{\vartheta\beta_{j}}V_{\rm loc}=-\varrho^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)
. Then the root-domination criteria is satisfied as

\displaystyle\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}V_{\rm loc}-\frac{\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\partial_{\vartheta\beta_{j}}V_{\rm loc}\right)^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{ab}

\displaystyle\qquad=\rmg^{\prime\prime}(\vartheta)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)-\frac{\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\varrho^{\prime\prime}(\beta_{j}-\vartheta)^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{ab}\geq\alpha-\frac{dR^{2}}{ab}\,.

The computation above verifies assumptions (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD
), with parameters given by
L_{V}^{\prime}\leftarrow 2(dR+L)
,
\ell_{V}^{\prime}\leftarrow\alpha-\frac{dR^{2}}{ab}
,
\ell_{V}\leftarrow ab
. Finally, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}(\beta)
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\left(\partial_{ij}V_{\rm loc}\right)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\mathcal{A}_{ij}^{2}(\beta)\;\leq\;\bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\mathcal{A}_{ij}^{2}(\beta)\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

Then,
Theorem

2.9
implies the conclusion.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.13
.

Note that
\varrho(t)=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\,t^{2}
,
\psi(t)=\frac{t^{2}}{2}
,
c(\sigma)=\sigma^{2}
. Then
\psi^{\prime\prime}=1
,
\varrho^{\prime\prime}=\tau^{2}
(note that this is stronger than the assumption
\varrho^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
in Theorem
2.12
),
\ell_{V}=a+\tau^{2}
, and

\displaystyle\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}V_{\rm loc}-\frac{\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\partial_{\vartheta\beta_{j}}V_{\rm loc}\right)^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}}{a+\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle=\rmg^{\prime\prime}(\vartheta)+d\tau^{2}-\frac{d\tau^{4}}{a+\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle=\rmg^{\prime\prime}(\vartheta)+\frac{ad\tau^{2}}{a+\tau^{2}}\geq\ell_{V}^{\prime}>0.

In addition,
L_{V}^{\prime}=2\,(d\tau^{2}+L)
. Applying
Theorem

2.9
yields the result.
∎

Lemma A.3

(Semi-log-concavity of a scale mixture of two Gaussians)
.

Let
\nu_{0},\nu_{1}
be normal densities with
\nu_{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}(0,\tau_{0}^{-2})
and
\nu_{1}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}(0,\tau_{1}^{-2})
, where
\tau_{1}^{2}<\tau_{0}^{2}
. For
\eta\in[0,1]
, define the function

\xi(x)\;\coloneqq\;-\log\left(\eta\,\nu_{0}(x)+(1-\eta)\,\nu_{1}(x)\right).

Then, its second derivative satisfies

\xi^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq\tau_{1}^{2}\;-\;2\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\log\Bigl(1+\frac{\eta\tau_{0}}{(1-\eta)e\tau_{1}}\Bigr)\,.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\coloneqq\frac{\eta}{1-\eta}
, and let
w\coloneqq\frac{\zeta\nu_{0}}{\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}
. Here we suppress the argument
x
for simplicity. For
k\in\{0,1\}
, a calculation shows that

\nu_{k}^{\prime}=-\tau_{k}^{2}x\nu_{k}\,,\qquad\nu_{k}^{\prime\prime}=\tau_{k}^{4}x^{2}\nu_{k}-\tau_{k}^{2}\nu_{k}\,.

Then, we have some useful identities:

(\nu_{k}^{\prime})^{2}-\nu_{k}\nu_{k}^{\prime\prime}=\tau_{k}^{2}\nu_{k}^{2}\,,\qquad 2\nu_{0}^{\prime}\nu_{1}^{\prime}-\nu_{1}\nu_{0}^{\prime\prime}-\nu_{0}\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}=-(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})^{2}x^{2}\nu_{0}\nu_{1}+(\tau_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2})\nu_{0}\nu_{1}\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{\zeta\nu_{0}^{\prime}+\nu_{1}^{\prime}}{\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}\Bigr)^{2}-\frac{\zeta\nu_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime}}{\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\zeta\nu_{0}^{\prime}+\nu_{1}^{\prime})^{2}-(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1})\,(\zeta\nu_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\nu_{1}^{\prime\prime})}{\bigl(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}\bigr)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{\zeta^{2}\tau_{0}^{2}\nu_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2}\nu_{1}^{2}+\zeta(\tau_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2})\nu_{0}\nu_{1}}{\bigl(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}\bigr)^{2}}}_{\coloneqq A_{1}}-\underbrace{\frac{\zeta(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})^{2}x^{2}\nu_{0}\nu_{1}}{\bigl(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}\bigr)^{2}}}_{\coloneqq A_{2}}\,.

We lower bound
A_{1}
by completing the square:

\displaystyle A_{1}=\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,(\zeta^{2}\nu_{0}^{2}-\nu_{1}^{2})}{2\,\bigl(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}\bigr)^{2}}=\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,(\zeta\nu_{0}-\nu_{1})}{2\,(\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1})}\,.

As
|\frac{\zeta\nu_{0}-\nu_{1}}{\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}|<1
, we can further bound
A_{1}
:

A_{1}\geq\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}+\tau_{1}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2}}{2}=\tau_{1}^{2}\,.

For
A_{2}
, we derive a bound in the following steps:

(a)

With
f(t)\coloneqq\frac{t}{\zeta\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})t\right)}
, we have

A_{2}\leq\frac{(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})^{2}x^{2}\zeta\nu_{0}}{\zeta\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}=(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})^{2}\zeta\tau_{0}f(x)\,.

(b)

Note that
\lim_{t\to\infty}f(t)=0
and
f(0)=0
. The maximum must be achieved at the stationary point
f^{\prime}(t)=0
. The first-order condition implies

\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t-1\bigr)\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t-1\Bigr)=\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{e\tau_{1}}\,.

Let
W(\cdot)
denote the Lambert
W
function, defined by
W(a)=u
when
u\exp(u)=a
. The optimal solution
t^{*}
solves

\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t^{*}-1=W\left(\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{e\tau_{1}}\right)\,.

Therefore, by the first-order condition, we have

\displaystyle\zeta\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t^{*}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,\tau_{1}t^{*}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t^{*}\Bigr)\,,

and

\tau_{1}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\,(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,t^{*}\Bigr)=\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{W\bigl(\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{e\tau_{1}}\bigr)}\,.

Plugging in
t^{*}
yields the maximum of
f
,

f(t^{*})=\frac{2}{(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\tau_{1}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})t^{*}\right)}=\frac{2W\bigl(\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{e\tau_{1}}\bigr)}{(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\zeta\tau_{0}}\,.

Thus,
A_{2}\leq(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})^{2}\zeta\tau_{0}f(t^{*})=2(\tau_{0}^{2}-\tau_{1}^{2})\,W\bigl(\frac{\zeta\tau_{0}}{e\tau_{1}}\bigr)
.

(c)

Since
\exp(\ln(1+z))-1=z
and
\exp(u)-1<u\exp(u)
,
W(z)\leq\log(1+z)
. We conclude with the desired bound.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.14
.

We again compute the requisite partial derivatives. The second partial derivative of
V_{\rm ss}
with respect to
\beta_{1}
is given by

\partial_{11}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\biggr]_{11}+\rmg^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\beta_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}\beta_{j}\Bigr)\,.

By our assumptions on
\psi
and
\rmg
, we have the following uniform bounds:

bA_{11}+\alpha\leq\partial_{11}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)\leq BA_{11}+L\,.

Thus, (
R
) is satisfied with
L_{V}^{\prime}=2\,(BA_{11}+L)
.

To continue, define
\zeta\coloneqq\eta/(1-\eta)
and
\xi(x)\coloneqq-\log\left(\eta\,\nu_{0}(x)+(1-\eta)\,\nu_{1}(x)\right)
. After some simplifications, one can compute the principal minor of the Hessian of
V_{\rm ss}
to be

\displaystyle\bigl[\nabla^{2}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)\bigr]_{-1}

\displaystyle=\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\biggr]_{-1}+{\rm{diag}}([\xi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\beta_{k})]_{k=2}^{d})+[\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}\rmg^{\prime\prime}]_{2\leq i,j\leq d}

\displaystyle\succeq bA_{-1}+{\rm{diag}}([\xi^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\beta_{k})]_{k=2}^{d})\succeq\ell_{V}I_{d-1}\,,

where the last two inequalities follow by our assumptions and
Lemma

A.3
.

We now verify (
RD
). For each
i\geq 2
, the mixed derivative is

\partial_{1j}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\biggr]_{1j}+\gamma_{j}\rmg^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\beta_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}\beta_{j}\Bigr)\,.

(43)

Since
ba_{d}I_{d}\preceq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\preceq Ba_{1}I_{d}
, the Schur complement theorem
(
119
, Theorem 1.6)
implies

\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\biggr]_{11}-ba_{d}-\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{d}\bigl(\bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}}{c(\sigma)}\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta)\bigr]_{1j}\bigr)^{2}}{Ba_{1}-ba_{d}}\geq 0\,.

(44)

For ease of notation, we omit
\beta
in the following expressions. Combining (
43
) and (
44
), we readily compute

\sum_{j=2}^{d}\left(\partial_{1j}V_{\rm ss}\right)^{2}\leq 2\left(Ba_{1}-ba_{d}\right)\left(BA_{11}-ba_{d}\right)+2\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}^{2}L^{2}\,,

thus

\displaystyle\partial_{11}V_{\rm ss}-\frac{\bigl\|\sum_{j=2}^{d}\left(\partial_{1j}V_{\rm ss}\right)^{2}\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}{ba_{d}}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq bA_{11}+\alpha-\frac{2\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}^{2}L^{2}}{ba_{d}}-\frac{2\left(Ba_{1}-ba_{d}\right)\left(BA_{11}-ba_{d}\right)}{ba_{d}}=\ell_{V}^{\prime}>0\,.

This verifies (
RD
). Now, computing the cross derivatives
\partial_{ij}V_{\rm ss}
for
i\neq j\geq 2
, we find

\partial_{ij}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(X_{k}^{\top}\beta)}{c(\sigma)}X_{ki}X_{kj}+\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}g^{\prime\prime}\Bigl(\beta_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}\beta_{j}\Bigr).

Finally, using the definition of
\mathcal{A}(\beta)
and
g{{}^{\prime\prime}}\leq L
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=2}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\left(\partial_{ij}V_{\rm ss}\right)^{2}\leq\Bigl\|\sum_{i=2}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\mathcal{A}_{ij}^{2}\Bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}+L^{2}\sum_{i=2}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\gamma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{2}\,.

We apply
Theorem

2.9
to yield the desired result.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.15
.

The full posterior is given by
\pi_{\rm ss}\propto\exp(-V_{\rm ss})
with

\displaystyle V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=\frac{\beta^{\top}A\beta}{2}-\sum_{j=2}^{d}\log\left(\eta\nu_{0}(\beta_{j})+(1-\eta)\nu_{1}(\beta_{j})\right)+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\,\Bigl(\beta_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}\beta_{j}\Bigr)^{2}\,.

In the remainder of the proof, we verify that assumptions (
R
), (
P1
), and (
RD
) hold with high probability.

The second-order partial gradient of
V_{\rm ss}
with respect to
\beta_{1}
is

\partial_{11}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=A_{11}+\tau^{2}
, thus (
R
) is satisfied with
L_{V}^{\prime}=2\,(A_{11}+\tau^{2})
.

We now verify (
P1
).
Let
\delta>0
be given.
By
103
, with probability at least
1-e^{-n\delta^{2}/2}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(A)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\min}(\bm{X})^{2}\geq(\sqrt{n\lambda_{d}}\,(1-\delta)-\sqrt{d\lambda_{1}})_{+}^{2}\,.

Take
\delta=0.9
. Since
\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{d}=o(n/d)
by assumption, for
n
sufficiently large, the lower bound is at least
0.8n\lambda_{d}
. That is,
A\succeq 0.8\,n\lambda_{d}I_{d}
.

For the full potential, this further implies
\nabla_{-1}^{2}V_{\rm ss}\succeq 0.8\,n\lambda_{d}I_{d-1}+D
, where
D
is a diagonal matrix that consists of the second derivatives of
-\log(\eta\nu_{0}(\beta_{j})+(1-\eta)\nu_{1}(\beta_{j}))
for
j=2,\dotsc,d
. By
Lemma

A.3
,
D\succeq c_{0}I_{d-1}
for some constant
c_{0}
independent of
d
and
n
. This implies that, for sufficiently large
n
, it holds that
\nabla_{-1}^{2}V_{\rm ss}\succeq\frac{2n\lambda_{d}}{3}\,I_{d-1}
.
Therefore, (
P1
) holds with the constant
\ell_{V}=\frac{2n\lambda_{d}}{3}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-0.4n}=1-o(1)
.

It remains to verify (
RD
). As
\partial_{1j}V_{\rm ss}(\beta)=A_{1j}+\tau^{2}\gamma_{j}
for
j\geq 2
and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, we know that

\displaystyle A_{11}+\tau^{2}-\frac{\frac{3}{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}(A_{1j}+\tau^{2}\gamma_{j})^{2}}{n\lambda_{d}}\geq\underbrace{A_{11}-\frac{3\sum_{j=2}^{d}A_{1j}^{2}}{n\lambda_{d}}}_{\eqqcolon D_{1}}+\underbrace{\tau^{2}-\frac{3\tau^{4}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}^{2}}{n\lambda_{d}}}_{\eqqcolon D_{2}}\,.

(45)

We proceed by bounding
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
separately.

By Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{d}A_{1j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{d}\langle e_{1},A\,e_{j}\rangle^{2}\leq 1.1\sum_{j=2}^{d}\langle e_{1},n\Sigma\,e_{j}\rangle^{2}+O\Bigl(\sum_{j=2}^{d}\langle e_{1},(A-n\Sigma)\,e_{j}\rangle^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 1.1n^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}+O\bigl(\left\|(A-n\Sigma)\,e_{1}\right\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\leq 1.1n^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}+O(\left\|A-n\Sigma\right\|_{2}^{2})\,.

By Theorem 6.1 and Example 6.3 in
103
,

\bigl\|n^{-1}\,A-\Sigma\bigr\|_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}\,(2\epsilon+\epsilon^{2})\,,

where
\epsilon\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}+\delta
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-n\delta^{2}/2}
. Then, setting
\delta=\sqrt{d/n}
, we see that with probability
1-o(1)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{d}A_{1j}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 1.1n^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}+O(dn\lambda_{1}^{2})\,.

(46)

On the other hand,
A_{11}
is a sum of
n
i.i.d.
\chi_{1}^{2}
random variables scaled by
\Sigma_{11}
, i.e.,
A_{11}
is a
\chi_{n}^{2}
random variable scaled by
\Sigma_{11}
.
By concentration of
\chi^{2}
random variables
(
103
, Example 2.5)
, it follows that
A_{11}\geq\frac{2}{3}\,n\Sigma_{11}
with high probability.

Combining with (
46
), we conclude that

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3}\,n\Sigma_{11}-\frac{3.3n^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}+O(dn\lambda_{1}^{2})}{n\lambda_{d}}\,.

Since
d\lambda_{1}^{2}/\lambda_{d}\ll n\lambda_{d}\ll n\Sigma_{11}
,
with probability
1-o(1)
, we derive that

\displaystyle D_{1}\geq\frac{n}{2}\,\Bigl(\Sigma_{11}-\frac{8\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}}{\lambda_{d}}\Bigr)=\ell_{V}^{\prime}\,.

(47)

Moving on to
D_{2}
. Recall that
D_{2}\coloneqq\tau^{2}-\frac{3\tau^{4}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}^{2}}{n\lambda_{d}}
.
By the preceding arguments,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{d}\gamma_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{j=2}^{d}A_{1j}^{2}}{A_{11}^{2}}\leq\frac{1.1n^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}+O(dn\lambda_{1}^{2})}{4n^{2}\Sigma_{11}^{2}/9}\,.

Therefore, for
\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{d}=o(\sqrt{n/d})
, as
\ell_{V}^{\prime}=\frac{n}{2}\,\Bigl(\Sigma_{11}-\frac{8\sum_{j=2}^{d}|\Sigma_{1j}|^{2}}{\lambda_{d}}\Bigr)>0
.

\displaystyle D_{2}\geq\tau^{2}-\frac{8\tau^{4}\sum_{j=2}^{d}\lvert\Sigma_{1j}\rvert^{2}}{n\lambda_{d}\Sigma_{11}^{2}}-O\Bigl(\frac{d\tau^{4}\lambda_{1}^{2}}{n^{2}\lambda_{d}^{3}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}+\frac{2\tau^{4}\ell_{V}^{\prime}}{n^{2}\Sigma_{11}^{2}}-\frac{\tau^{4}}{n\Sigma_{11}}-o\Bigl(\frac{\tau^{4}}{n\lambda_{d}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\tau^{2}-O\Bigl(\frac{\tau^{4}}{n\lambda_{d}}\Bigr)\,.

(48)

Hence,
D_{2}\geq 0
with probability
1-o(1)
provided
\tau^{2}=o(n\lambda_{d})
.

By combining (
45
), (
47
), and (
48
), (
RD
) is satisfied with the defined
\ell_{V}^{\prime}
with high probability. Finally, applying
Theorem

2.9
with
L_{V}^{\prime}=2\,(A_{11}+\tau^{2})
,
\ell_{V}=\frac{n\lambda_{d}}{2}
, and
\ell_{V}^{\prime}
, and noting that
\partial_{ij}V_{\mathrm{ss}}(\beta)=A_{ij}+\tau^{2}\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}
for
i\neq j\geq 2
, we obtain

\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}_{\rm ss}\parallel\pi_{\rm ss}\right)\leq\frac{8\,(A_{11}+\tau^{2})}{n^{2}\ell_{V}^{\prime}\lambda_{d}^{2}}\sum_{i=2}^{d}\sum_{j>i}\left(A_{ij}^{2}+\tau^{4}\gamma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{2}\right)\,.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs from Section
3

Proof of
Proposition

3.1
.

Consider two elements
T,\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
. We aim to show
\lambda T+(1-\lambda)\tilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})\sim\rho
and denote
Y\coloneqq T(X)
,
Z\coloneqq\tilde{T}(X)
. It is equivalent to show that
{\rm Law}(\lambda Y+(1-\lambda)Z)\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
.

Let
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq\{2,\ldots,d\}
be two sets such that
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\emptyset
. As
\rho=\mathcal{N}(0,I)
, we know that
X_{S_{1}}\perp X_{S_{2}}
, where
X_{S_{l}}\coloneqq(X_{j})_{j\in S_{l}}
for
l\in\{1,2\}
.
Thus, as

\displaystyle\lambda Y_{i}+(1-\lambda)Z_{i}=\lambda T_{i}\left(X_{i}\mid T_{1}(X_{1})\right)+(1-\lambda)\tilde{T}_{i}(X_{i}\mid\tilde{T}_{1}(X_{1}))

for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,d\}
, we conclude that

\left\{\lambda Y_{i}+(1-\lambda)Z_{i}\right\}_{i\in S_{1}}\perp\left\{\lambda Y_{j}+(1-\lambda)Z_{j}\right\}_{j\in S_{2}}\mid X_{1}\,.

(49)

Furthermore, since
\lambda T_{1}+(1-\lambda)\tilde{T}_{1}
is strictly increasing, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\lambda Y_{1}+(1-\lambda)Z_{1}
is the same as the one generated by
X_{1}
. Combining with (
49
) implies

\left\{\lambda Y_{i}+(1-\lambda)Z_{i}\right\}_{i\in S_{1}}\perp\left\{\lambda Y_{j}+(1-\lambda)Z_{j}\right\}_{j\in S_{2}}\mid\left(\lambda Y_{1}+(1-\lambda)Z_{1}\right).

Hence, applying the Hammersley–Clifford theorem
(see, e.g.,
103
, Theorem 11.8)
shows the result.
∎

Before presenting the proof of
Theorem

3.2
, we provide an alternative proof of the existence and uniqueness of the solution to (
6
), relying on the convexity of the equivalent problem (

T

-

SSVI

) and the stability of conditional independence under convergence in
L^{2}(\rho)
over
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
. A recent work by
12
discusses the close relation between the topology induced by star-separable maps (and more generally, Knothe–Rosenblatt maps) and the one induced by the adapted Wasserstein distance. The latter is finer than the information topology
(
7
)
, which preserves conditional independence.

The
adapted
(
2
-)Wasserstein distance is defined as

\displaystyle AW_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{\rm bc}(\mu,\nu)}\int\|x-y\|^{2}\,\pi({\rm d}x,{\rm d}y)\,,

(50)

where we call
\Pi_{\rm bc}(\mu,\nu)
the set of
bicausal
couplings between
\mu
and
\nu
. This set consists of
\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
such that

\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x_{k},\mathrm{d}y_{k}\mid x_{1:k-1},y_{1:k-1})\in\Pi\left(\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{k}\mid x_{1:k-1}),\nu(\mathrm{d}y_{k}\mid y_{1:k-1})\right),\quad\pi\text{-a.s.}

for all
k\leq d
, i.e.,

\pi(\mathrm{d}x_{1:d},\mathrm{d}y_{1:d})=\pi_{1}(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}y_{1})\,\pi_{2}(\mathrm{d}x_{2},\mathrm{d}y_{2}\mid x_{1},y_{1})\dotsm\pi_{d}(\mathrm{d}x_{d},\mathrm{d}y_{d}\mid x_{1:d-1},y_{1:d-1})\,.

Remark B.1
.

To connect with star-separable maps, we observe that for any
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
with
{T_{1}}_{\#}\rho=\mu
and
{T_{2}}_{\#}\rho=\nu
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
is defined in
Section

3.1
, then
(T_{1},T_{2})_{\#}\rho\in\Pi_{{\rm bc}}(\mu,\nu)
. Thus,
AW_{2}(\mu,\nu)\leq\left\|T_{1}-T_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}
.

Definition B.2

(Hellwig’s information topology)
.

Define the family of maps
\{\mathcal{I}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d-1}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d-k}))\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}(\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{K}^{k}_{\#}\mu\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{k+1},\ldots,\mathrm{d}x_{d}\mid x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\right)\,.

Hellwig’s information topology is the coarest topology such that
\mathcal{I}_{k}
is continuous for all
k\leq d-1
, where the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d-k}))
is endowed with the usual topology of weak convergence.

The following theorem says that the topology induced by the adapted Wasserstein distance is finer than the information topology.

Theorem B.3

(
7
)
.

Convergence in the adapted Wasserstein distance is equivalent to convergence in the information topology plus convergence of the second moment.

Finally, we recall the following fact which implies that convergence in the adapted Wasserstein distance preserves conditional independence.

Theorem B.4

(
10
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
be Polish spaces,
\{(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z})
. Suppose that
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in the information topology and
\nu_{n}\to\nu
weakly. Let
\mu_{n}^{x}
denote the transition kernel of
\mu_{n}
w.r.t. the first marginal. Then,

\nu_{n}\otimes\mu_{n}^{x}\to\nu\otimes\mu^{x}\quad\text{weakly}\,.

Given
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})
, let
\pi_{xy}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
(resp.
\pi_{xz}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z})
) denote the pushforward of
\pi
by the projection operator
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,y)
(resp.
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,z)
).

Proposition B.5
.

Given a sequence of probability measures
\{\pi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})
such that
\pi_{n}\to\pi
weakly, suppose that
\pi_{n}=\pi_{n,xz}\otimes\pi_{n,xy}^{x}
, i.e.,
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}y\mid x,z)=\pi_{n}(\mathrm{d}y\mid x)
, and
\pi_{n,xy}\to\pi_{xy}
in the adapted Wasserstein distance. Then,
\pi=\pi_{xz}\otimes\pi_{xy}^{x}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

B.3
,
\pi_{n,xy}\to\pi_{xy}
in the information topology. Applying
Theorem

B.4
to
\mu_{n}=\pi_{n,xy}
and
\nu_{n}=\pi_{n,xz}
, the result follows from the uniqueness of the weak limit.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

Convexity:

This is similar to the proof that the KL divergence with respect to a strongly log-concave measure is strongly convex along generalized geodesics, see
3
.

Let
T_{0},T_{1}\in\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
, define
T_{t}\coloneqq(1-t)T_{0}+tT_{1}
, and
\mu_{t}\coloneqq T_{t\#}\rho
.
We now compute the second derivative of

\displaystyle t\mapsto\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{t}\parallel\pi\right)=\mathcal{V}(\mu_{t})+\mathcal{H}(\mu_{t})\coloneqq\int V\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}+\int\log\mu_{t}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\,.

The following two equalities can be easily verified
(see, e.g.,
100
, Chapter 5)
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{V}(\mu_{t})}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}=\int\bigl\langle\nabla^{2}V(T_{t})\,(T_{1}-T_{0}),\,T_{1}-T_{0}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\rho,

(51)

and

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\mu_{t})}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}=\int\|(DT_{t})^{-1}D(T_{1}-T_{0})\|^{2}_{\rm F}\,\mathrm{d}\rho\,.

(52)

It follows from (
SLC
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\KL(\mu_{t}\mathbin{\|}\pi)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\,\|T_{1}-T_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}^{2}\,,

which is what we wanted to show.

Existence and uniqueness:
Let
\{\mu^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
be a minimizing sequence, i.e.,
\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{n}\parallel\pi\right)\to a\coloneqq\inf_{\mu\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)
as
n\to\infty
. For every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
T^{m}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
(resp.
T^{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
) denote the star-separable map from
\rho
to
\mu^{m}
(resp.
\mu^{n}
). By
Proposition

3.1
,
\mu^{m,n}\coloneqq(\frac{1}{2}T^{m}+\frac{1}{2}T^{n})_{\#}\rho\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
, thus
\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{m,n}\parallel\pi\right)\geq a
and
\liminf_{m,n\to\infty}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{m,n}\parallel\pi\right)\geq a
. By
\alpha
-convexity of
T\mapsto\mathsf{KL}\left(T_{\#}\rho\parallel\pi\right)
, we have

\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{m,n}\parallel\pi\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{m}\parallel\pi\right)+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu^{n}\parallel\pi\right)-\frac{\alpha}{8}\,\|T^{m}-T^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\rho)}\,.

As
\alpha>0
, taking
\liminf_{m,n\to\infty}
on both sides implies that

\limsup_{m,n\to\infty}\|T^{m}-T^{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\rho)}=0\,.

Therefore, by completeness of
L^{2}(\rho)
, there exists
T^{\star}\in L^{2}(\rho)
such that
T^{n}\to T^{\star}
in
L^{2}(\rho)
. Denote by
\pi^{\star}\coloneqq T^{\star}_{\#}\rho
, then
\mu^{n}\to\pi^{\star}
converges in Wasserstein distance and thus converges weakly. Combining with the weak lower semi-continuity of
\mu\mapsto\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\parallel\pi\right)
, we derive that
\mathsf{KL}\left(\pi^{\star}\parallel\pi\right)\leq a
.

We claim that
\pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{C}_{\rm star}
, whence, we conclude that
\pi^{\star}
is a minimizer to (
3
). Indeed, by the Hammersley–Clifford theorem
(
103
, Theorem 11.8)
, it suffices to verify that for any
1<j\leq d
,
\pi_{j}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mid z_{1},\ldots,z_{j-1})=\pi_{j}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mid z_{1})
. Since
T^{n}\to T^{\star}
in
L^{2}(\rho)
, we know that
AW_{2}(\mu^{n}_{\{1,j\}},\pi^{\star}_{\{1,j\}})\to 0
as the measurability is preserved under
L^{2}(\rho)
convergence. Therefore, applying
Proposition

B.5
with
\pi_{n}=\mu^{n}_{[j]}
and
\pi=\pi^{\star}_{[j]}
yields the claim.

Finally, the uniqueness of
T^{\star}
follows from the
\alpha
-convexity and the convexity of
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
, which also shows the uniqueness of
\pi^{\star}
.
∎

In fact, the key is the convergence of
T_{n}
to
T^{\star}
in
L^{2}(\rho)
, as this convergence preserves conditional independence. This also shows that the
L^{2}(\rho)
-closure of
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
is also convex. Therefore, we can conclude that a minimizer exists over any set of structured distributions if the set of lifted maps is convex, which gives potential guidance for other structured VI problems.

Appendix C
Computational guarantees

C.1
Regularity of star-separable maps

In this subsection, we provide the proofs of
Lemma

3.4
and
Theorem

3.5
.

Lemma

3.4
establishes a bound on the mixed derivative of the SSVI minimizer. As this result is used repeatedly in the subsequent proofs, we restate it below for convenience.

Lemma C.1
.

Let (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD

+

) be satisfied. Denote by

L\coloneqq\max_{i\geq 2}\sup_{z_{1},z_{i}\in\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{1}\partial_{i}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})|\,.

Then

L\leq\overline{L}\coloneqq\frac{\ell_{V}\max_{i\geq 2}\|\partial_{1i}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}-\max_{i\geq 2}\bigl\|\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}|\partial_{ij}V|\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}<+\infty\,.

Proof of
Lemma

3.4
.

The proof uses the strategy of self-bounding. Denote
h_{i}(z_{1},z_{i})\coloneqq-\partial_{1}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z_{1})
.
Theorem

2.4
yields that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}h_{i}(z_{1},z_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]

\displaystyle+\int\partial_{i}V(z_{1},z_{i},z_{-\{1,i\}})\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\partial_{1}\log\rmq^{\star}_{j}(z_{j}\mid z_{1})\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\partial_{i}V(Z)\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\Bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\Bigr]\,.

Therefore, as
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\mid Z_{1}=z_{1}]=0
for all
j\geq 2
,
i\neq j
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}h_{i}(z_{1},z_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]\right|

(53)

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\Bigl[\partial_{i}V(Z)\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\Bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\Bigr]\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\bigl[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1},Z_{i},Z_{j}\right]h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\bigr]\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\cov_{\pi^{\star}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1},Z_{i},Z_{j}],h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\bigr)\Bigr|\,.

Using the asymmetric Brascamp–Lieb inequality
(
26
, Theorem 1.1)
with
p=\infty
,
q=1
, and noting that
-\partial_{jj}\log\rmq_{j}^{\star}(z_{j}\mid z_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}[\partial_{jj}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{j}=z_{j}]\geq\ell_{V}
by
Theorem

2.4
, we derive that for every
j\neq i\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\left|\cov_{\pi^{\star}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\partial_{i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1},Z_{i},Z_{j}],h_{j}(Z_{1},Z_{j})\bigm|Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\bigr)\right|

(54)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\int\left|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}\left[\partial_{ij}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i},Z_{j}=z_{j}\right]\right|\rmq_{j}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{j}\mid z_{1})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{z_{j}\in\mathbb{R}}{\Bigl|\frac{\partial_{j}h_{j}(z_{1},z_{j})}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{jj}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{j}=z_{j}\right]}\Bigr|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{\ell_{V}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\left|\partial_{ij}V(Z)\right|\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right].

Combining (
53
) and (
54
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}h_{i}(z_{1},z_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\partial_{1i}V(Z)\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{L}{\ell_{V}}\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\left|\partial_{ij}V(Z)\right|\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right].

By the triangle inequality, we derive that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{i}h_{i}(z_{1},z_{i})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{L}{\ell_{V}}\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\left|\partial_{ij}V(Z)\right|\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}}\left[\left|\partial_{1i}V(Z)\right|\mid Z_{1}=z_{1},Z_{i}=z_{i}\right].

Taking the supremum over
z_{1},z_{i}
and then maximum over
i\geq 2
on both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle L-\max_{i\geq 2}\|\partial_{1i}V\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{L}{\ell_{V}}\max_{i\geq 2}{\Bigl\|\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}|\partial_{ij}V|\Bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}\,.

Under (
RD

+

), the inequality above implies a bound on
L
:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ell_{V}\max_{i\geq 2}\|\partial_{1i}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}-\max_{i\geq 2}\bigl\|\sum_{j\geq 2,\,j\neq i}|\partial_{ij}V|\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}=\overline{L}\,.

∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD

+

) be satisfied. Let
T^{\star}
be the optimal star-separable map from
\rho
to
\pi^{\star}
.
Then, for any
z_{1},\tilde{z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{d}\int|T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid\tilde{z}_{1})-T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})|^{2}\,\rho_{i}\left(\mathrm{d}x_{i}\right)\leq\frac{\bigl\|\sum_{i\geq 2}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\,|\tilde{z}_{1}-z_{1}|^{2}\,.

(55)

Proof.

Since
x_{i}\mapsto T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})
is increasing for any
z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, by
Lemma

A.1
we know that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{d}\int|T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid\tilde{z}_{1})-T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})|^{2}\,\rho_{i}\left(\mathrm{d}x_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{d}W_{2}^{2}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}_{1}),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}))

\displaystyle=W_{2}^{2}(\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\bigl\|\sum_{i=2}^{d}|\partial_{1i}V|^{2}\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\,|\tilde{z}_{1}-z_{1}|^{2}\,,

where the second line follows from the fact that
\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}_{1}),\,\rmq^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes(d-1)}
.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Assume (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD

+

) hold. For all
z_{1},\tilde{z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i\geq 2
,
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})-T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid\tilde{z}_{1})\right|\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}}\,|\tilde{z}_{1}-z_{1}|\,,

(56)

i.e.,
z_{1}\mapsto T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})
is
\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}}
-Lipschitz in
z_{1}
uniformly for all
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We drop the subscripts in
x_{i},z_{1},\tilde{z}_{1}
for brevity. We first make the following key observation:

\left|T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid z)-T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid\tilde{z})\right|\leq W_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))\qquad\text{for all}\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(57)

Here
W_{\infty}
denotes the
\infty
-Wasserstein distance between
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined by

W_{\infty}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\|d\|_{L^{\infty}(\pi)},

where
d(x,y)=\|x-y\|
and
L^{\infty}(\pi)
denotes the
L^{\infty}
norm under
\pi
.

To see this, we claim that the above inequality holds for
\rho_{1}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then by the continuity of
T_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z)
and
T_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z})
, the inequality holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

We now validate the claim using contradiction. Let
\delta>0
. Suppose the inequality fails over a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}
with
\rho_{1}(E)>0
, such that

\left|T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid z)-T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid\tilde{z})\right|\geq W_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))+\delta\,,\qquad x\in E\,.

Then, because
(T_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),T_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))_{\#}\rho_{1}
is a
W_{p}
-optimal coupling of
\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z)
and
\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z})
,

\displaystyle W_{p}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))

\displaystyle\geq\Bigl(\int_{E}|T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid z)-T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid\tilde{z})|^{p}\,\rho_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)\Bigr)^{1/p}

(58)

\displaystyle\geq\bigl(W_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))+\delta\bigr)\,\rho_{1}(E)^{1/p}\,,

where
W_{p}
is the
p
-Wasserstein distance. Letting
p\to\infty
, as
W_{p}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))\to W_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))
when
p\to\infty

(see, e.g.,
46
, Proposition 3)
, we arrive at the contradiction.

Therefore, combining (
57
), Theorem 2.5 in
58
, and
Lemma

3.4
, we have

\displaystyle\left|T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid z)-T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid\tilde{z})\right|

\displaystyle\leq W_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z),\,\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\,I_{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z)\mathbin{\|}\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ell_{V}}\,\|(\log\rmq_{i}^{\star})^{\prime}(\cdot\mid z)-(\log\rmq_{i}^{\star})^{\prime}(\cdot\mid\tilde{z})\|_{L^{\infty}(\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}}\,|z-\tilde{z}|\,,

where for
\nu\ll\mu
, the
L^{\infty}
relative Fisher information is given by

\displaystyle I_{\infty}(\nu\mathbin{\|}\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl\|\nabla\log\bigl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\bigr)\bigr\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\,.

∎

Remark C.4
.

Lemma

C.3
implies that
z_{1}\mapsto T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})
is differentiable in
z_{1}
almost everywhere for all
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.

We will make use of the following standard facts throughout the next part of the proof; see
55
for proofs.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
T
denote the optimal transport map from
\rho=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
to
\mu
, and let
m
denote the mean of
\mu
. If
T^{\prime}\leq\beta
, then
|T(0)-m|\leq\sqrt{2/\pi}\,\beta
.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
m
and
\tilde{m}
denote the mean and the mode of
\mu
, respectively, where
\mu
is
\ell_{V}
-log-concave and univariate.
Then,
|m-\tilde{m}|\leq 1/\sqrt{\ell_{V}}
.

Lemma C.7
.

Assume (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD

+

) hold. For all
i\geq 2
,
x_{i},z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,

|\partial_{z_{1}}\partial_{x_{i}}T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid z_{1})|\lesssim\frac{L_{V}\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\,(1+|x_{i}|)\,.

(59)

Proof of
Lemma

C.7
.

For ease of notation, we fix
i
and drop the subscripts in
z_{1},x_{i}
. Denote by
T_{z}(x)\coloneqq T_{i}^{\star}(x\mid z)
and
\rmq_{z}
the density of
\rmq_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z)
. Taking the log of the change of variables formula gives

\displaystyle\log\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)=-\log\sqrt{2\pi}-\frac{x^{2}}{2}-\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, taking the derivative w.r.t.
z
yields that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{z}\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)}{\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)}=-\frac{\partial_{x}\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))\,\partial_{z}T_{z}(x)}{\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))}-\frac{\partial_{z}\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))}{\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))}\qquad\text{for all}\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}\,.

Rearranging to isolate
\partial_{z}\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)
yields

\partial_{z}\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)=\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)\,\bigl(-\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))\,\partial_{z}T_{z}(x)-\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))\bigr)

(60)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Here,
\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))
should be understood as the derivative of
\log\rmq_{z}(\cdot)
evaluated at the point
T_{z}(x)
.
By
\ell_{V}
-log-concavity of
\rmq_{z}
and Caffarelli’s contraction theorem,
T_{z}
is
\sqrt{1/\ell_{V}}
-Lipschitz, i.e.,
0\leq\partial_{x}T_{z}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}
. On the other hand, by
Lemma

C.3
,
|\partial_{z}T_{z}(x)|\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}}
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\partial_{z}\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)\,\bigl(-\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))\,\partial_{z}T_{z}(x)-\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,\Bigl(|\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))|\,\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}}+|\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))|\Bigr)\,.

Denote by
\tilde{m}_{z}
and
m_{z}
the mode and mean of
\rmq_{z}
, respectively. Since
\rmq_{z}
is
L_{V}
-log-smooth by
Theorem

2.8
, and
T_{z}
is smooth (in
x
), we can apply
Lemma

C.5
and
Lemma

C.6
and obtain

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{|\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(\tilde{m}_{z})|}_{=0}+L_{V}\,|T_{z}(x)-\tilde{m}_{z}|

\displaystyle\leq L_{V}\left(|T_{z}(x)-T_{z}(0)|+|T_{z}(0)-m_{z}|+|m_{z}-\tilde{m}_{z}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq L_{V}\,\Bigl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,|x|+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{L_{V}}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,(1+|x|)\,.

We proceed with bounding
\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))
. Note that by the fundamental theorem of calculus and as
|\partial_{z}\partial_{x}\log\rmq_{z}(x)|\leq\overline{L}
, we have (again, using
Lemma

C.5
and
Lemma

C.6
)

\displaystyle|\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(T_{z}(x))|

\displaystyle\leq|\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(\tilde{m}_{z})|+\overline{L}\,(|T_{z}(x)-T_{z}(0)|+|T_{z}(0)-m_{z}|+|m_{z}-\tilde{m}_{z}|)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(\tilde{m}_{z})|+\frac{\overline{L}}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,(1+|x|)\,.

By
Theorem

2.4
, the derivative
\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(z_{i})=\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}(z_{i}\mid z)
satisfies

\displaystyle-\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(z_{i})

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\Bigl(\int V(z,z_{i},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z)\Bigr)

(61)

\displaystyle+\partial_{z}\log\int\exp\Bigl(-\int V(z,z_{i}^{\prime},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}\,.

Note that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\int\exp\Bigl(-\int V(z,z_{i}^{\prime},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad=-\int\partial_{z}\Bigl(\int V(z,z_{i}^{\prime},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z)\Bigr)\,\rmq^{\star}_{i}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}\mid z)\,.

Denoting

F(z,z_{i})\coloneqq\partial_{z}\Bigl(\int V(z,z_{i},z_{-\{1,i\}})\,\rmq_{-i}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}z_{-\{1,i\}}\mid z)\Bigr)\,,

we can rewrite (
61
) as

\displaystyle-\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(z_{i})

\displaystyle=F(z,z_{i})-\int F(z,z_{i}^{\prime})\,\rmq^{\star}_{i}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}\mid z)

\displaystyle=\int\bigl(F(z,z_{i})-F(z,z_{i}^{\prime})\bigr)\,\rmq^{\star}_{i}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}\mid z)\,.

Moreover, by
Lemma

3.4
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{z_{i}}F(z,z_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\partial_{z_{i}}\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{i}^{\star}\left(z_{i}\mid z\right)\right|\leq\overline{L}\,.

Therefore, the mean value theorem and
31
yield that

\displaystyle|\partial_{z}\log\rmq_{z}(\tilde{m}_{z})|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{L}\int|\tilde{m}_{z}-z_{i}^{\prime}|\,\rmq^{\star}_{i}(\mathrm{d}z_{i}^{\prime}\mid z)\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,.

Combining the above displays, we conclude:

|\partial_{z}\partial_{x}T_{z}(x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,\Bigl(\frac{L_{V}\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,(1+|x|)+\frac{\overline{L}}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\,(1+|x|)+\frac{\overline{L}}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}\Bigr)\lesssim\frac{L_{V}\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}^{2}}\,(1+|x|)\,.

∎

We conclude this subsection with the proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

The first part of
Theorem

3.5
follows along the lines of the proof of
55
. Then, the bound on
\partial_{z_{1}}T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid\cdot)
is shown in
Lemma

C.3
, and the bound on
\partial_{z_{1}}\partial_{x_{i}}T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid\cdot)
is shown in
Lemma

C.7
.
∎

C.2
Dictionary of maps

To continue the development from
Section

3.4
, we now design a parameterized family of star-separable transport maps
\mathcal{T}_{\Theta}
to approximate the SSVI minimizer. The construction is based on a finite dictionary of star-separable maps
\mathcal{M}
, and we consider a polyhedral subset of
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
defined by the conic hull
{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M})
. Following
55
, the set
{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M})
is constructed as

\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\Bigl\{\sum_{T\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda_{T}T\,\,\Big|\,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|\mathcal{M}|}\Bigr\}\,,

where
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
is the non-negative orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{M}|}
. Clearly, when
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
,
\mathrm{cone}(\mathcal{M})
is a convex cone in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
.

We now construct the dictionary
\mathcal{M}
from one-dimensional piecewise linear maps. Define
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\psi(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}0\,,&x\leq 0\,,\\
x\,,&x\in[0,1]\,,\\
1\,,&x\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

The function
\psi
is the basic building block of our dictionary. Let
R>0
, partition the interval
[-R,R]
into
N=2R/\delta
sub-intervals
‡‡
‡‡

‡‡

We assume that
R
is a multiple of
\delta
and omit the explicit dependence of
\mathcal{M}
on
\delta
and
R
for brevity.
of length
\delta>0
, and let the set of endpoints be
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{N+1}\}
with
b_{1}=-R
and
b_{N+1}=R
. For convenience, we denote by
\mathcal{B}=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{N}\}
and set
b_{0}\coloneqq b_{1}
,
b_{N+2}\coloneqq b_{N+1}
. The function classes are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\,e_{1}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,b\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

(62)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b^{\prime}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in[b^{\prime},b^{\prime}+\delta)\right\}}\,e_{i}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,i\geq 2,\,b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\psi\Bigl(1-\frac{x_{1}-b^{\prime}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in[b^{\prime},b^{\prime}+\delta)\right\}}\,e_{i}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,i\geq 2,\,b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\geq R\right\}}\,e_{i}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,i\geq 2,\,b\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}<-R\right\}}\,e_{i}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,i\geq 2,\,b\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{5}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Bigl\{x\mapsto\pm\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b}{\delta}\Bigr)\,e_{i}\,\,\Bigl|\,\,i\geq 2,\,b\in\mathcal{B}\Bigr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{0}\cup\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{M}_{5}\,.

We note that
\mathcal{M}_{0}
consists of non-decreasing piecewise linear functions of
x_{1}
. The maps in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
are piecewise linear maps depending jointly on
x_{i}
and
x_{1}
, which are non-decreasing over the set
(x_{1},x_{i})\in[b^{\prime},b^{\prime}+\delta)\times\mathbb{R}
.
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is built on
\mathcal{M}_{1}
by flipping the sign of
x_{1}
. Each element of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is a a monotonically increasing function in
x_{i}
but monotonically decreasing in
x_{1}
for
x_{1}\in[b^{\prime},b^{\prime}+\delta)
. Finally,
\mathcal{M}_{3},\mathcal{M}_{4},\mathcal{M}_{5}
are designed to approximate
T_{i}^{\star}
when
x_{1},x_{i}\notin[-R,R]
.

We further enrich
\mathcal{M}
with constant coordinate shifts
\pm e_{i}
,
i\in[d]
to handle tail behavior. Equivalently, we define the
augmented cone
:

\underline{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\Bigl\{\sum_{T\in\mathcal{M}}\lambda_{T}T+v\Bigm|\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{|\mathcal{M}|},\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\Bigr\}\,.

Finally, by
Theorem

3.5
, for
z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\{2,\dots,d\}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1/L_{V}^{\prime}}\leq\left|\partial_{x_{1}}T_{1}^{\star}(\cdot)\right|\leq\sqrt{1/\ell_{V}^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\sqrt{1/L_{V}}\leq\left|\partial_{x_{i}}T_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid z_{1})\right|\leq\sqrt{1/\ell_{V}}\,.

Define
\bm{\alpha}\coloneqq\big((L_{V}^{\prime})^{-1/2},L_{V}^{-1/2},\dots,L_{V}^{-1/2}\big)
.
We arrive at the
\bm{\alpha}
-augmented-and-spiked cone
:

\underline{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M};\,\bm{\alpha}\id)\coloneqq\bm{\alpha}\,{\id}+\underline{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M})\,.

(63)

Thus,
\underline{\rm cone}(\mathcal{M};\,\bm{\alpha}\id)\subset\mathcal{T}_{\rm star}
.

C.2.1
The approximation procedure

We now show that the dictionary in the previous subsection is such that there exists an approximator
\widehat{T}\in\underline{\rm cone}\left(\mathcal{M};\,\bm{\alpha}\,{\mathrm{id}}\right)
which approximates
T^{\star}
to arbitrary precision (see
Corollary

C.12
).

Recall that the optimal star-separable map has the structure

T^{\star}(x)=\left[T^{\star}_{1}(x_{1}),T^{\star}_{2}(x_{2}\mid T^{\star}_{1}(x_{1})),\dotsc,T^{\star}_{d}(x_{d}\mid T^{\star}_{1}(x_{1}))\right]\qquad\text{for all}\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

For clarity, we write
T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i};x_{1})\coloneqq T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i}\mid T^{\star}_{1}(x_{1}))
to emphasize the dependence of
T_{i}^{\star}
on
x_{1}
.

To approximate
T^{\star}
, we first approximate
T^{\star}_{1}
by a univariate piecewise linear function
\widehat{T}_{1}
. For
i\geq 2
, we approximate
T_{i}^{\star}(x_{i};x_{1})
by decoupling its dependence on
x_{i}
and
x_{1}
and constructing approximators for both parts separately.

We restrict
(x_{i},x_{1})
to
[-R,R]\times[-R,R]
for all
i\geq 2
.

To approximate the map
T_{1}^{\star}
, we define

\widehat{T}_{1}(x_{1})\coloneqq T_{1}^{\star}(-R)+\sum_{m=1}^{N}\lambda_{m}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,,

(64)

where the non-negative coefficients
\lambda_{m}\coloneqq T_{1}^{\star}(b_{m+1})-T_{1}^{\star}(b_{m})\geq 0
encode the local increments of
T_{1}^{\star}
along the partition grid.

We next construct an approximation for
T_{i}^{\star}
with
i\geq 2
. For each endpoint
b_{j}\in\mathcal{B}\cup\{b_{N+1}\}
, define the localized approximation as

\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})\coloneqq T_{i}^{\star}(-R;b_{j})+\sum_{m=1}^{N}\lambda_{m,j}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,,

(65)

where
\lambda_{m,j}\coloneqq T_{i}^{\star}(b_{m+1};b_{j})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{m};b_{j})\geq 0
.

For
x_{1}\in I_{j}=[b_{j},b_{j+1})
,
j\in[N]
, we interpolate between adjacent approximation maps:

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{b_{j+1}-x_{1}}{\delta}\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})+\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})

(66)

\displaystyle=\psi\Bigl(1-\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})+\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})

\displaystyle=\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})+\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\bigl(\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})-\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})\bigr)\,.

When
x_{i}\in I_{j}
,
j\in\{0,N+1\}
, we define

\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})\coloneqq\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})\,.

Combining all the above, we conclude the construction of the approximator
\widehat{T}_{i}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})

\displaystyle=\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};-R)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}<-R\right\}}+\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};R)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\geq R\right\}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\biggl(\psi\Bigl(1-\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})+\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})\biggr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in[b_{j},b_{j+1})\right\}}

\displaystyle=T_{i}^{\star}(-R,-R)+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\bigl(T_{i}^{\star}(-R;b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(-R;b_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{m=1}^{N}\biggl\{\lambda_{m,0}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}<-R\right\}}+\lambda_{m,N+1}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\geq R\right\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{m,j}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\psi\Bigl(1-\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in[b_{j},b_{j+1})\right\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{m,j+1}\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{m}}{\delta}\Bigr)\,\psi\Bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\Bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{x_{1}\in[b_{j},b_{j+1})\right\}}\biggr\}\,.

We remark that the proposed piecewise linear interpolation yields a continuous approximation in
x_{1}
while preserving monotonicity in
x_{i}
. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that
\widehat{T}\in\underline{{\rm cone}}(\mathcal{M})
.

Lemma C.8
.

Assume (
R
), (
P1
), (
P2
), and (
RD

+

) hold. For
i\geq 2
, the approximator
\widehat{T}_{i}
defined under (
65
)-(
66
) satisfies the following:

(i)

When
x_{1}\in I_{0}\cup I_{N+1}
,
\partial_{x_{1}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})=0
.

(ii)

When
x_{i}\in I_{0}\cup I_{N+1}
,
\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})=0
.

(iii)

For all
x_{i},x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,

|\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})|\leq 1/\sqrt{\ell_{V}}\quad\text{and}\quad|\partial_{x_{1}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})|\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}\sqrt{\ell_{V}^{\prime}}}\,.

Furthermore,

|\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{1}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})|\leq\Bigl(\frac{L_{V}}{\ell_{V}^{3/2}}+\frac{L_{V}\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}^{2}\sqrt{\ell_{V}^{\prime}}}\Bigr)\,(1+|x_{i}|+\delta)\,.

Proof.

(i) and (ii) hold trivially by construction. We now show that (iii) holds.
Let
\eta_{k,j}\coloneqq T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j})
. Note the following identity:

\lambda_{k,j}+\eta_{k+1,j}=T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j})=\lambda_{k,j+1}+\eta_{k,j}\,.

Let
\Delta_{j,k}\coloneqq\lambda_{k,j+1}-\lambda_{k,j}=\eta_{k+1,j}-\eta_{k,j}
. For all
x_{1}\in I_{j}
,
x_{i}\in I_{k}
, we first compute the partial gradients of
\widehat{T_{i}}
w.r.t.
x_{1},x_{i}
explicitly. Write
u_{k}(x_{i})=\psi\bigl(\frac{x_{i}-b_{k}}{\delta}\bigr)
and
w_{j}(x_{1})=\psi\bigl(\frac{x_{1}-b_{j}}{\delta}\bigr)
, then

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})-\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})}{\delta}

\displaystyle=(1-u_{k}(x_{i}))\,\frac{T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j})}{\delta}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+u_{k}(x_{i})\,\frac{T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j})}{\delta}

and

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})

\displaystyle=(1-w_{j}(x_{1}))\,\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j})+w_{j}(x_{1})\,\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};b_{j+1})

\displaystyle=(1-w_{j}(x_{1}))\,\frac{T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j})}{\delta}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+w_{j}(x_{1})\,\frac{T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j+1})}{\delta}\,.

Using the definitions of
\lambda_{k,j}
and
\eta_{k,j}
, we obtain the forms

\partial_{x_{1}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})=\frac{\eta_{k,j}}{\delta}+u_{k}(x_{i})\,\frac{\Delta_{j,k}}{\delta}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(x_{i};x_{1})=\frac{\lambda_{k,j}}{\delta}+w_{j}(x_{1})\,\frac{\Delta_{j,k}}{\delta}\,.

(67)

By
Theorem

3.5
and the mean value theorem, we derive that

0\leq\frac{\lambda_{k,j}}{\delta}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell_{V}}}

(68)

and for some
\eta_{j}\in[b_{j},b_{j+1}]
,

\Bigl|\frac{\eta_{k,j}}{\delta}\Bigr|=\frac{1}{\delta}\,\bigl|T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j})\bigr|\leq\bigl|\partial_{2}T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k}\mid T_{1}^{\star}(\eta_{j}))\bigr|\,|\partial_{x_{1}}T_{1}^{\star}(\eta_{j})|\leq\frac{\overline{L}}{\ell_{V}\sqrt{\ell_{V}^{\prime}}}\,.

(69)

Similarly, for some
\xi_{k},\chi_{k}\in I_{k}
,
\beta_{k}
between
\xi_{k},\chi_{k}
, and
\gamma_{j}\in[b_{j},b_{j+1}]
,

\displaystyle\Bigl|\frac{\Delta_{j,k}}{\delta}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\lambda_{k,j+1}-\lambda_{k,j}}{\delta}\right|=\left|\frac{T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j+1})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j+1})-(T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k+1};b_{j})-T_{i}^{\star}(b_{k};b_{j}))}{\delta}\right|
